This angle connects to the broader beat because federal funding often supports workforce training, research, and other initiatives that affect regional education and economic development. Understanding how federal compliance requirements work and what happens when institutions fail to disclose foreign affiliations provides important context for covering higher education on the Shore.

---

## Higher-Education and Student-Parent Support

Beyond federal compliance, a new dimension of higher-education coverage is emerging around student-parent support services. Columnist Mike Munoz at the Star-Democrat authored "Student-parents need support from colleges," drawing on a case study from Long Beach City College in California, where a comprehensive "one-stop shop" model provides childcare, emergency financial aid, and other services tailored to students balancing parenting and coursework. This model offers a benchmark for evaluating whether Eastern Shore institutions—particularly Chesapeake College and Salisbury University, which serve many non-traditional students—are adequately supporting student-parents.

Reporters should investigate what support services currently exist at regional colleges for student-parents, what gaps remain, and what barriers prevent broader adoption of comprehensive support models. This angle connects to workforce development and economic mobility: if community colleges can better support student-parents, more adults may be able to complete credentials and advance their careers. Profiles of student-parents navigating college while raising families can illustrate the real-world challenges and bring policy questions to life.

---

## State Accountability and Equity Data

The Maryland State Department of Education released the 2024 Maryland School Report Card on December 3, 2024. Overall, 41 percent of schools earned four- or five-star ratings, up from 38 percent the prior year. However, disaggregated data showed persistent gaps for Black, Hispanic, multilingual, economically disadvantaged, and special-education students. Deputy State Superintendent Geoff Sanderson and State Superintendent Cary Wright emphasized moving toward more granular, student-by-student accountability and revising standards for English language arts, math, science, and health. For Eastern Shore reporters, the report card offers a statistical backbone for school-level stories, equity investigations, and tracking the local impact of Blueprint for Maryland's Future. Reporters should examine how Shore schools performed on the 2024 report card, investigate whether equity gaps identified statewide are visible in local data, and profile how individual schools are addressing disparities.

---

## Capital Funding and Chesapeake College

Chesapeake College is pursuing a major capital project: replacing its Queen Anne's Tech building. This facility serves the technical and workforce training needs of students across the five-county Mid-Shore Community College District, making it essential infrastructure for the region's economic development. The project carries a total price tag of approximately $79 million and spans a three-year construction timeline.

The funding model reveals a structural tension in how Maryland finances higher education. The state will support 75 percent of project costs, and the counties will support the remaining 25 percent. This arrangement forces local elected officials to choose between competing priorities in tight budget years. Caroline County's deliberation over its contribution illustrates the calculus: commissioners must weigh immediate capital needs against future debt service, all while managing K-12 education funding, transportation, and other services. Kent and Queen Anne's counties face similar decisions, and reporters should monitor how each jurisdiction justifies its vote to constituents. In April 2025, counties approved a revised cost-sharing arrangement, with Queen Anne's County covering Dorchester County's share of design costs, demonstrating regional problem-solving in the face of fiscal constraints.

Early evidence suggests the new facility is already generating real-world educational returns. During February and March 2025, construction activity at Chesapeake College's Tech Building provided hands-on learning opportunities for Queen Anne's County High School (QACHS) Career and Technical Education program (CTE) students. In collaboration with Chesapeake College, carpentry and masonry CTE students completed construction of a mock house. This collaboration underscores how the college's new tech facility will deepen the college-and-career pipeline that BMF emphasizes and provides concrete evidence of the investment's value as counties debate their funding contributions.

This story has multiple angles worth pursuing. One is the decision itself: whether each county ultimately approves its share and what factors drive those decisions. Another is the broader impact. Local employers and students can speak to how a modern technical facility might shift enrollment patterns and job placement rates. A third angle explores state policy: how Maryland's higher-education capital-fund formula aligns or conflicts with county budget cycles, particularly in fiscally constrained years. The Queen Anne's Tech project is also a window into workforce development priorities on the Shore—what skills the region needs and how education institutions are preparing students to meet that demand.

Clifford Coppersmith, president of Chesapeake College, is the public face of the Queen Anne's Tech project and broader workforce training initiatives on the Shore. He can discuss capital funding, enrollment trends, and employer partnerships.

---

## The Blueprint for Maryland's Future and Local Implementation

The state's Blueprint for Maryland's Future is reshaping education across the Shore. The initiative mandates teacher pay raises, early learning expansion, and other reforms. However, implementation has been subject to significant recent changes. In April 2025, lawmakers agreed to a revised bill that maintained funding for community schools, implemented a three-year delay to policy requirements for collaborative time, and established an automatic freeze in annual funding increases if state revenue drops by roughly $1 billion. These changes followed intense negotiations between the administration, both chambers of the legislature, and education stakeholders.

The 2024 Talbot County board race offered a clear articulation of local perspectives on BMF. Candidates emphasized concerns about state mandates limiting local flexibility for successful programs that may not align perfectly with state priorities. This critique reflects a broader tension between state-level reform and local control—a theme that appears in board meetings, candidate platforms, and community conversations across all three counties.

Coverage should explore how each district is managing this tension. Which BMF mandates are districts implementing fully? Where are implementation challenges most acute? What does the state reform look like at ground level, where teachers and administrators are managing competing demands and limited resources? Profiles of teachers or administrators navigating these tensions can bring the policy debate to life. Matthew Kibler's role overseeing BMF implementation in Queen Anne's County before becoming interim superintendent makes him a valuable source for understanding how districts approach state reform initiatives.

---

## Emerging Budget and Fiscal Issues

School budgets across the Shore are facing pressure from multiple directions simultaneously. Rising health-care costs, delayed state funding, and capital project obligations are creating a fiscal squeeze that deserves sustained coverage.

Health-care costs represent a fiscal pressure affecting multiple districts. Queen Anne's County's insulin-price litigation illustrates how pharmaceutical costs can strain district budgets. The lawsuit, filed in October 2024, challenges inflated insulin prices that directly affect the district's health-benefit budget for employees. The litigation underscores how rising health-care expenses compete with educational spending. Reporters should investigate whether similar health-care cost pressures exist in other Shore districts and whether districts are exploring cost-containment strategies or seeking legislative relief.

Delayed state funding compounds these pressures. Queen Anne's County's financial audit, initiated in 2024, follows board frustration over state payments that often do not arrive until June, forcing districts to manage cash flow challenges throughout the fiscal year. As this audit proceeds, it may reveal systemic issues affecting other Shore districts as well. Reporters should monitor the audit's findings and consider whether similar budget pressures exist in Talbot, Caroline, and Dorchester.

The intersection of delayed state payments, rising health-care expenses, and capital project obligations creates a fiscal squeeze that deserves investigation across multiple districts. What strategies are superintendents and finance directors using to manage these competing pressures? Are there systemic solutions at the state or county level? Rising lunch-debt balances represent another emerging fiscal challenge affecting district budgets and student access to meals. Reporters should investigate how widespread lunch debt is across Shore districts, what policies districts have for collecting debt, and what impact unpaid lunch balances have on student access to nutrition and family finances.

The piggy-backing procurement strategy used by Queen Anne's County offers a model worth investigating in other districts. In June 2024, the QACPS Board approved piggy-backing on Cecil County Public Schools' existing contracts for bread and paper products, representing significant cost savings. This approach demonstrates how smaller districts can leverage shared services to reduce costs and improve buying power. Reporters should investigate whether other Shore districts are exploring similar arrangements and what barriers might prevent adoption.

Dorchester County's $10 million budget deficit and subsequent cuts to after-school and summer programming illustrate how fiscal pressures cascade through districts. The loss of school-run programming heightened reliance on nonprofits and community organizations to fill the gap. Reporters should investigate whether similar budget pressures and program cuts are occurring in other districts and what impact these cuts have on students and families, particularly those with fewer resources to access private alternatives.

---

## Chronic Absenteeism and Student Attendance

Chronic absenteeism has emerged as a statewide policy focus, and Mid-Shore counties are reporting rates between 22.9 percent and 38.3 percent. This represents a significant post-pandemic challenge affecting student learning and district operations. Reporters should investigate what districts are doing to address absenteeism, what barriers students face to regular attendance, and what support services exist for chronically absent students. This story connects to broader questions about student mental health, family economic hardship, transportation access, and the effectiveness of intervention strategies. Tracking how districts are responding to state pressure on absenteeism provides an opportunity to examine whether current approaches are working or whether new resources and strategies are needed.

---

## Federal Immigration Policy and School Climate

Federal immigration policy shifts are creating new challenges for Shore schools. In January 2025, Superintendent Pepukayi's letter to Talbot County families reaffirming school safety for all students regardless of immigration status came in response to the Trump administration's rescission of the 2018 "sensitive locations" policy. The letter's emphasis on ACLU resources and partnerships with the Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center signals how districts are building support networks for undocumented and immigrant families in politically volatile moments.

Reporters should track whether other Shore districts issue similar communications and investigate whether immigration enforcement activity is affecting enrollment or student attendance. This angle extends education coverage beyond traditional classroom reporting and illuminates how federal policy decisions have immediate local consequences. Questions worth pursuing include: How are districts communicating with immigrant families about their rights? Are there documented cases of students or families leaving the Shore due to immigration concerns? What partnerships are districts building with legal-aid organizations and immigrant-advocacy groups?

---

## Parent Advocacy Groups and School Board Politics

The intersection of parent-advocacy groups and school-board politics deserves sustained attention. Moms for Liberty, a conservative parents' rights organization, has chapters in 10 Maryland counties and is actively recruiting school board candidates. Conservative candidates backed by parent-rights organizations and the 1776 Project PAC saw notable success in the 2024 school board election cycle across Maryland, including in Talbot County. Reporters should track Moms for Liberty and 1776 Project PAC activity across all three Shore counties. Which candidates do they endorse? What specific policy changes are they seeking? How do their priorities align or conflict with BMF mandates and student-support services? This reporting could reveal whether parent-rights advocacy is reshaping education policy on the Shore and what that means for curriculum decisions, counseling services, and other student support.

The political dynamics around school boards also involve questions about intellectual freedom and book challenges. Librarians and educators on the Shore advocate for open access to information, while parent-advocacy groups push for opt-out processes and removal of materials they deem inappropriate. Editorial voices like Missy Corley's library advocacy represent an important counterweight to parent-rights pressure. Reporters should investigate how the state's opt-out law is being implemented in Shore schools, what materials have been challenged, and how boards are navigating these disputes. This angle reveals the tension between parental authority and professional educator judgment—a tension that often surfaces in board meetings and candidate campaigns.

---

## Alternative Educational Models

The emergence of microschool and hybrid homeschooling options represents a new dimension of Shore education coverage. Beyond Words Tutoring, founded by special-education teacher Lindsey Parte, launched a hybrid homeschooling microschool in Stevensville beginning September 2024, serving 6-11-year-olds with blended schedules and low student-to-teacher ratios. This development reflects growing interest in alternative educational pathways beyond traditional public schools and raises questions about equity, access, and the role of specialized programs in the broader education ecosystem. Reporters should investigate what drives families to seek alternative models, what outcomes these programs achieve, and how they interact with or complement traditional district offerings.

---

## Technology and Discipline Policies

The locked-phone pouch initiative offers a window into how districts are responding to national concerns about digital citizenship and instructional focus. The Yondr system, piloted at Easton Middle School in December 2024, has gained rapid adoption across the Shore and is expanding nationally, with at least 2.5 million students using pouches across 35 states as of 2025. The policy has divided parents: some praise the focus it creates; others worry about emergency access and the durability of the pouches. District officials stress that emergency-unlock devices exist and that student advisory input is mixed.

Reporters should track compliance rates across districts, investigate equity implications for students with disabilities or emergency communication needs, and document student and parent perspectives on the policies as implementation continues. This reporting can illuminate broader questions about digital citizenship, instructional focus, and how schools are managing technology in ways that serve student learning. The story also connects to concerns about screen time, student mental health, and the role of technology in education—themes that resonate with both parents and educators.

---

## Key Contacts and Sources

**Superintendents and District Leadership**

Sharon Pepukayi, Superintendent of Talbot County Public Schools, can discuss mental-health initiatives, student wellness programs, partnerships with external organizations, technology-discipline policies, immigration policy impacts, and how federal policy shifts affect school climate and student enrollment.

Derek Simmons, Superintendent of Caroline County Public Schools, can discuss board-culture issues, budget pressures, environmental-education initiatives, and technology-discipline policies.

Jymil Thompson, Superintendent of Dorchester County Public Schools, is leading the district following leadership transitions in 2024. He can discuss district priorities, instructional leadership focus, how the district is managing governance changes, and the county-wide literacy strategy.

Matthew Kibler, Interim Superintendent of Queen Anne's County Public Schools, can discuss the district's transition planning, Blueprint implementation, budget pressures affecting district operations, and his previous work overseeing the district's accountability and implementation efforts.

**Board Leadership**

Richard Barton, President of the Caroline County Board of Education, can provide perspective on board dynamics.

Sheri Hubbard, President of the Dorchester County Board of Education, leads the reconstituted board following the 2024 resignations and can discuss board priorities and governance recovery.

Emily Jackson, President of the Talbot County Board of Education, can discuss board decisions and district strategic planning.

**County Government**

Travis Breeding and Larry Porter, Caroline County Commission President and Vice President respectively, are central figures in the Chesapeake College funding decision and broader county budget discussions affecting education.

**Literacy and Early Learning**

Matthew Peterson, Executive Director of the John & Janice Wyatt Foundation, can discuss the funding strategy behind the Dorchester County Campaign for Grade Level Reading and broader philanthropic support for education on the Shore.

Bryant Wittington, Director of the Dorchester County Campaign for Grade Level Reading, can discuss hub programming, volunteer recruitment, and progress toward moving the district's literacy ranking from 21st to 15th.

Tynice DeShields, Director of Mental Health at the YMCA of Chesapeake, can discuss the Robbins YMCA Reading Hub, early-childhood literacy programming, and the connection between early literacy and mental-health outcomes.

Ashley Singer, Executive Director of Open Doors Partners in Education, can discuss Orton-Gillingham literacy tutoring, community-partnership models, nonprofit resilience following the facility fire, and expansion plans across the Eastern Shore.

Dr. Carol Franks-Randall, retired educator and children's-book author, provides expert commentary on literacy instruction and the role of illustrated books in supporting struggling readers.

**Community Organizations and Nonprofits**

Judy Corchiarino, Executive Director of Queen Anne's County Advocates for Youth, can discuss nonprofit youth development, volunteer governance, and partnerships between schools and community organizations.

Lt. Maryjoe Phillips, Unit liaison for the U.S. Naval Sea Cadet Corps Maryland Eastern Shore Unit, can discuss military-linked youth programs, civic education, and volunteer recruitment challenges.

David Murray, Operations Manager and Head Chef at the Chesapeake Culinary Center, and Angel Perez, Programming and Events Manager, can discuss community-based adult education, food security, and plans for expansion across the Eastern Shore.

Keasha Haythe, Executive Director of the Foundation of HOPE and Talbot County Council member, can discuss youth development programs for middle-school girls, nonprofit expansion, and the new facility project on South Washington Street in Easton.

**Environmental Education**

Julie Strange and Zellie Kneller, program coordinators for the Green-School certification initiative at Denton Elementary and Greensboro Elementary respectively, can discuss the two-year certification process, curriculum redesign, community partnerships, and the impact of environmental education on student learning.

**College Leadership**

Clifford Coppersmith, President of Chesapeake College, is the public face of the Queen Anne's Tech project and broader workforce training initiatives on the Shore. He can discuss capital funding, enrollment trends, and employer partnerships.

**Curriculum and Instruction**

Jodi Colman, Director of Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment at Dorchester County Public Schools, can discuss early-grade assessment data, post-pandemic student readiness, and curriculum initiatives.

**Communications and Outreach**

Valerie Goff, Communications and Community Outreach Specialist at Dorchester County Public Schools, is the primary contact for information about district communications and initiatives.

**Food Services**

Julie Hickey, Coordinator of Food Services for Queen Anne's County Public Schools, can discuss the transition from Sodexo to self-operational food service and efforts to support regional farms and preserve employment.

**Facilities and Operations**

Kevin Shafer, Director of Operations for Talbot County Public Schools, oversees the Chapel District Elementary renovation and can discuss school facility projects, construction timelines, and capital funding strategies.

**Media and Community Voices**

Sharon Harrington, Easton Star-Democrat editorial writer, has authored reporting on parent-advocacy groups and school-board candidates. Her reporting provides context for understanding the political landscape in Talbot County.

Missy Corley, editorial voice and library advocate, represents important perspectives on intellectual freedom and the opt-out process for contested materials.

Julie Quick, organizer with the Mid-Shore Constitution Alliance, can discuss civic-education initiatives, the Constitution Alliance events, and plans for expanding outreach to Shore schools.

Wes Hagood, President of the Sons of the American Revolution – Talbot County Chapter, can discuss the Constitution Bowl and other civic-education partnerships with schools.

**Higher Education and Federal Compliance**

Josh Shannon, Star-Democrat reporter, covered the University of Delaware federal compliance settlement and can discuss the case and implications for regional institutions.

David Weiss, U.S. Attorney, District of Maryland, commented on the UD settlement and can discuss federal grant oversight procedures.

Adelle Harris, Special Agent, NASA Inspector General, provides insight on federal research oversight and compliance procedures.
=== education_beat_book_refined_v2.md ===
# Education Beat Book – Easton Star-Democrat
## Maryland's Eastern Shore

This guide equips reporters with the people, institutions, issues, and story ideas shaping education coverage on the Eastern Shore. It is updated as new reporting unfolds and should serve as a launchpad for deep-dive reporting, quick-turn pieces, and long-form features.

---

## Understanding the Eastern Shore Education Landscape

The Eastern Shore's education system spans three county school districts—Talbot, Caroline, and Dorchester—plus Chesapeake College, the regional community college serving five Mid-Shore counties. This geography matters for coverage because decisions at the state and county level often intersect, particularly around capital funding, workforce training, and implementation of the Blueprint for Maryland's Future, the state's education reform initiative.

The beat encompasses several interconnected layers of coverage: governance transitions and board dynamics, budget pressures from health-care costs and delayed state funding, technology and discipline policies, mental-health and violence-prevention initiatives, environmental education and hands-on STEM, civic engagement programs, federal immigration policy impacts on school climate, post-pandemic student readiness, alternative educational models, arts programming, workforce development, higher-education initiatives, school facility capital projects, and community partnerships filling gaps left by school budget constraints.

---

## Board Politics and Governance

Board politics shifted significantly in November 2024. The Talbot County school board election saw three newcomers win seats, reflecting broader concerns about how state education reforms are being implemented locally. Candidates backed by parent-rights organizations saw success in Talbot and other Shore districts. Reporters should investigate whether current board members have ties to parent-advocacy organizations and how their priorities align with Blueprint mandates and support services for vulnerable students.

Dorchester County stabilized its board following dual resignations in June 2024. Board President Mike Diaz and Vice President Susan Morgan both resigned. Brandy Dawson Cumberland and Christine LaMonica were appointed to fill vacated seats. The board is now led by President Sheri Hubbard, with Vice President Talibah Chikwendu. Jymil Thompson was appointed superintendent, officially beginning July 1, 2024.

Caroline County is led by Superintendent Derek Simmons, appointed effective July 1, 2022. In March 2024, board member Donna DiGiacomo resigned following a public clash with Superintendent Simmons over workplace culture issues, raising important questions about superintendent-board relations that reporters should monitor.

---

## Childhood Literacy and Early-Learning Deficits

Childhood literacy is a significant emerging challenge on the Eastern Shore, though recent data show progress. Students in Dorchester County saw English scores rise from 33.7% to 41.3% in the 2024-25 school year, representing substantial gains. Third-grade reading ability is a national predictor of high school graduation; early gaps compound over a child's academic trajectory.

In response to budget pressures, Dorchester County has pursued an innovative community-partnership model for early childhood development. The Robbins YMCA Reading Hub, operated in partnership with the county schools, public library, United Way of the Lower Eastern Shore, and Operation Pathways, serves as a resource center for early-childhood reading groups for prenatal mothers, infants, toddlers, and preschoolers. This partnership emerged partly out of necessity. Dorchester County Public Schools entered the 2025 budget cycle with a $10 million deficit, prompting cuts to after-school and summer programming. The hub represents a deliberate effort to move beyond traditional school-based tutoring toward a comprehensive, community-embedded approach to early literacy.

Public libraries have become central to this ecosystem. Large-scale book festivals and author visits draw families into public libraries, often in partnership with programs like Dolly Parton's Imagination Library. Open Doors Partners in Education, a nonprofit based in Chestertown serving Kent County and surrounding areas, provides free and low-cost Orton-Gillingham literacy tutoring from pre-K through adult learners. In 2025, the nonprofit experienced a significant setback when fire destroyed its facility and materials, resulting in approximately $40,000 in losses. Despite this crisis, the organization rapidly relocated and is executing a five-year strategic plan for expansion across the Eastern Shore.

---

## Environmental Education and Hands-On STEM

Environmental education has emerged as a growth area for Shore schools. Two Caroline County elementary schools—Denton Elementary and Greensboro Elementary—earned Maryland Association for Environmental and Outdoor Education Green-School certification in 2025. The certification process required a two-year application and curriculum redesign with partnerships from Pickering Creek Audubon Center, Chesapeake Bay Foundation, and Tuckahoe State Park. Reporters should track whether other Shore schools are pursuing similar certification.

STEM engagement is expanding through workplace partnerships. Kent County High School operates a sophomore STEM job-shadowing program that places students in daylong tours of local businesses. The partnership links classroom learning directly to real-world occupational pathways. Reporters should monitor longitudinal outcomes for participants—college enrollment, apprenticeship uptake, and employer hiring of program graduates—to assess the program's impact.

---

## Technology, Discipline, and Student Engagement

A significant technology-discipline initiative emerged at Easton Middle School in December 2024. Principal Kelly Murdoch piloted locked-phone pouches (Yondr brand), reporting higher student engagement and fewer multitasking interruptions. The policy has already been adopted by Caroline and Dorchester districts and is under consideration for expansion in Talbot County. Yondr boasts that millions of students from all 50 states are now using its pouches.

In August 2025, Dorchester County's Board of Education approved a district-wide policy that prohibits personal electronic devices for the entire school day. Devices must be turned off and stored; violations can lead to confiscation and discipline. Board President Sheri Hubbard highlighted a high schooler's testimony that phone-free lunches fostered real conversation. The board reviewed 115 public comments before voting.

These device policies have created genuine tensions. Parents are divided: some praise the focus they create; others worry about emergency access. Reporters should track compliance rates across districts, investigate equity implications for students with disabilities or emergency communication needs, and document student and parent perspectives as implementation continues.

Discipline data from Talbot County offers an important baseline for ongoing coverage. As of October 2025, the district reported zero expulsions over the previous three years and steady out-of-school suspension rates, suggesting a focus on restorative-justice approaches.

---

## Mental-Health Initiatives and Violence Prevention

Mental-health support has emerged as a cross-cutting priority across Shore education. In October 2023, international motivational performer Alex Boyé toured Talbot County Public Schools under the "You Are a 10!" program, delivering talks on self-worth, resilience, and mental-health awareness to students from 5th through 12th grade, funded by the MD Aware II grant.

Violence prevention has become an emerging priority. Dorchester County Public Schools is piloting Risk Threat Crisis' Violence Prevention Initiative at North Dorchester High School, with support from the nonprofit Someone to Walk With. The program trains students, staff, and parents to recognize warning signs and intervene early. Rebecca Redmer, founder of Someone to Walk With and co-creator of the Violence Prevention Initiative, is a key source for understanding community-based approaches to school safety and grant-seeking strategies. Reporters should track the pilot's first-semester outcomes and the program's expansion to other schools and districts.

---

## Capital Planning and School Facilities

Capital projects are reshaping the Eastern Shore's educational infrastructure. In Caroline County, the Board of Education approved a FY 2027-30 capital-improvement program that earmarks significant funding for a new Lockerman Middle School. The Ed Specs Committee recommended the North Caroline High School property as the preferred site, citing infrastructure benefits and cost savings. The total projected cost is $103.82 million, with roughly $80 million expected from the state and the remainder from local sources. Superintendent Derek Simmons indicated the county's ask to the state will be trimmed after "research of student population and other factors."

The recommendation emphasized several advantages of the North Caroline site: clear paths for indoor and outdoor activities, the absence of street crossings, and the ability to share the high school's large auditorium and gym for events. Development on the North Carolina site would be approximately $500,000 cheaper than the alternative Double Hills Road site. An existing solar array at the site would cost roughly $180,000 to purchase and relocate, though traffic on Central Avenue is a noted community concern that warrants further investigation.

Reporters should track the negotiation process between Caroline County and the Maryland State Department of Education to determine how much of the projected cost will ultimately become a local burden. The demographic trends and enrollment projections that drive capital-needs estimates deserve investigation. Comparing Caroline's capital-improvement timeline with other Eastern Shore districts will reveal regional patterns in school-building funding. Additional story angles include examining how co-locating a middle school with a high school could affect student outcomes and transportation logistics, investigating the feasibility and cost-benefit of acquiring the existing solar array versus installing a new system, and assessing traffic impact on Central Avenue.

In Talbot County, Kevin Shafer, Director of Operations, is overseeing a multi-million-dollar renovation of Chapel District Elementary School. Reporters should track the project timeline, budget, and any construction-related impacts on student instruction.

---

## Talbot County Public Schools

Talbot County is led by Superintendent Sharon Pepukayi, who has served since July 2022. At the July 16, 2025 board meeting, the Talbot County Board of Education approved a five-year strategic plan and began consolidating retirement-plan providers—a cost-saving measure reflecting broader budget pressures. The board also announced plans for a new staff health center slated to open in October 2025. In May 2025, the district cut summer programming due to grant loss, and notably, the school board was excluded from the decision-making process. This detail raises important questions about superintendent authority, budget decision-making processes, and board oversight.

In January 2025, Superintendent Pepukayi issued a letter to families reaffirming that schools remain "safe places" for all students, regardless of immigration status, after the Trump administration rescinded the 2018 "sensitive locations" policy. Reporters should track whether other Shore districts issue similar communications and investigate whether immigration enforcement activity is affecting enrollment or student attendance.

The Talbot County Education Foundation honored three distinguished alumni in November 2024—Keasha Haythe (Foundation of Hope president and Talbot County Council member), JoAnn "Asparagus" Murray (former family-law magistrate), and Dr. Ludwig Eglseder III (local physician). The ceremony raised funds for six teacher-requested projects, including a student-leadership program, pre-K language and literacy initiative, and Easton Middle School band. Superintendent Pepukayi emphasized the importance of teachers being able to pitch ideas and secure external support. This represents a broader story opportunity: tracking how alumni-driven fundraising translates into concrete programmatic outcomes and examining the role of external funding in supplementing strained school budgets.

---

## Caroline County Public Schools

Caroline County's arts programming offers a consistent reporting opportunity. The district publishes a detailed concert schedule each December, featuring student performances at venues like North Caroline High School and Colonel Richardson High School. A notable partnership emerged in July 2025 when Ashton Mooday, a local singer-songwriter and music teacher, collaborated with Washington College to give ten Chestertown middle-school students a professional recording experience. The college's audio-engineer students Stevie Lyles and Mariana Kilman operated the studio equipment, allowing the students to record a finished track in a professional setting. Mooday is planning to expand her music instruction facility to accommodate summer camps and homeschooling classes, reflecting growing community demand for expanded arts education. This collaboration illustrates how local arts educators can leverage higher-education resources to deliver high-impact experiential learning and points to an emerging story angle: mapping college-K-12 partnerships across the Eastern Shore and identifying gaps where similar collaborations might be replicated.

---

## Dorchester County Public Schools

Dorchester County provides a window into post-pandemic student readiness and strategic reform. In 2024, the district reported a 7-percentage-point jump in kindergarten readiness, with students "demonstrating" readiness rising from 32 percent to 39 percent. Language, literacy, and math scores improved, but social-foundation and physical-well-being metrics slipped.

Superintendent Jymil Thompson, who began in July 2024, has launched an ambitious strategic plan aimed at moving the district from 23rd to 15th in state rankings within four years. The plan emphasizes instructional leadership, academic improvement, operational optimization, partnerships, and community engagement. Early results show promise, with the district climbing in state rankings after the first year of implementation.

---

## The Blueprint for Maryland's Future and Local Implementation

The state's Blueprint for Maryland's Future is reshaping education across the Shore. In 2025, lawmakers approved compromise legislation to trim the Blueprint for Maryland's Future while keeping much of the multiyear plan intact. The Excellence in Maryland Public Schools Act delays implementation of "collaborative time," preserves funding for community schools and protects students in poverty, those in special education and those in classes for non-English speakers from any potential per pupil funding cuts.

The 2024 Talbot County board race offered a clear articulation of local perspectives on the Blueprint. Candidates emphasized concerns about state mandates limiting local flexibility. This critique reflects a broader tension between state-level reform and local control—a theme that appears in board meetings, candidate platforms, and community conversations across all three counties. Reporters should monitor how this tension plays out as the Blueprint rolls forward.

---

## Emerging Budget and Fiscal Issues

School budgets across the Shore are facing pressure from multiple directions simultaneously. Rising health-care costs, delayed state funding, and capital project obligations are creating a fiscal squeeze that deserves sustained coverage.

Delayed state funding compounds budget pressures. Queen Anne's County's financial audit, initiated in 2024, follows board frustration over state payments that often do not arrive until June, forcing districts to manage cash flow challenges throughout the fiscal year. Reporters should monitor the audit's findings and consider whether similar budget pressures exist in Talbot, Caroline, and Dorchester. The Dorchester County example—a $10 million deficit forcing cuts to summer and after-school programming—shows how budget pressures cascade into reduced services for students most dependent on school-based support.

---

## Workforce Development and Higher Education

Chesapeake College is pursuing a major capital project: replacing its Queen Anne's Tech building. The state will support 75 percent of project costs, and the counties will support the remaining 25 percent. The proposed new building will take three years to build and will house programs in agriculture, electrical, welding and fabrication, construction trades, and advanced manufacturing.

The college's winter programming—including CDL information sessions, virtual skilled-trades info sessions, and entry-level marine-service-technician and engine-service classes—illustrates how a two-year institution partners with local industry and workforce agencies to create pipelines for students. Reporters should monitor enrollment, completion rates, and employer feedback to gauge the effectiveness of these workforce pathways.

Chesapeake College is actively pursuing Hispanic-Serving Institution (HSI) status, a designation that unlocks federal grant funding. The college has experienced rapid growth in Hispanic enrollment—up 64 percent over the last decade and now representing more than 43 percent of the college's minority students. The college has translated all communications into Spanish, hired Spanish-speaking staff, and partnered with Talbot County Public Schools family liaisons for FAFSA-night community events that achieved a 90 percent application rate. This trajectory demonstrates how demographic shifts are reshaping higher-education priorities on the Shore and creating new grant-seeking opportunities for institutions. Reporters should track the college's HSI application timeline and what federal funding might become available if the designation is granted. Clifford Coppersmith, President of Chesapeake College, is a key source for discussing capital funding, enrollment trends, workforce programming, employer partnerships, and HSI initiatives.

Washington College, located in Chestertown on the Kent County side of the Shore, opened "The Golden Goose" pub in early 2025 inside the former Hynson Lounge. The college applied for a liquor license amendment. Restrictions include "21-plus only," ID checks, wristbands, and a ban on outside alcohol. The pub is not open to the public; students may bring two guests. This development illustrates the intersection of campus-life enhancements, local regulatory oversight, and student-safety concerns. Reporters should monitor compliance checks and any incidents that arise during the first year of operation.

---

## Community Organizations and Youth Development

Beyond the three county school districts, a network of nonprofit organizations and civic groups shape education and youth development on the Eastern Shore. Queen Anne's County Advocates for Youth, a volunteer-run nonprofit coordinated by Executive Director Judy Corchiarino, coordinates programs and projects for local youth and families.

The U.S. Naval Sea Cadet Corps operates the Maryland Eastern Shore Unit, a youth development program serving ages 10-18. Unit liaison Lt. Maryjoe Phillips oversees a program that teaches naval customs, leadership, discipline, and citizenship. The program has grown from 5 cadets in 2018 to 29 in 2024, demonstrating sustained community interest in military-linked youth development.

The Foundation of HOPE, a nonprofit serving middle-school girls in Talbot County, represents another dimension of community-based youth development. Founded in 2017, the organization runs an after-school program focused on economic development, entrepreneurship, and self-confidence. Executive Director Keasha Haythe is leading an expansion effort that includes construction of a new 5,200-square-foot building in Easton. The project has a total cost of $2.2 million, with $1.8 million already raised.

The Chesapeake Culinary Center, a nonprofit adult-education program based in Denton, Caroline County, teaches cooking, budgeting, meal-prep, and food-preservation skills through free and fee-based workshops. Operations Manager and Head Chef David Murray leads a program that offers classes in English, Spanish, and Haitian Creole and has plans to expand to other Eastern Shore counties.

---

## Chronic Absenteeism and Student Attendance

Chronic absenteeism has emerged as a statewide policy focus, and Mid-Shore counties are reporting rates between 22.9 percent and 38.3 percent. This represents a significant post-pandemic challenge affecting student learning and district operations. Reporters should investigate what districts are doing to address absenteeism, what barriers students face to regular attendance, and what support services exist for chronically absent students.

---

## Federal Immigration Policy and School Climate

Federal immigration policy shifts are creating new challenges for Shore schools. In January 2025, Superintendent Pepukayi's letter to Talbot County families reaffirming school safety for all students regardless of immigration status came in response to the Trump administration's rescission of the 2018 "sensitive locations" policy. The letter's emphasis on support networks for undocumented and immigrant families signals how districts are building support systems in politically volatile moments. Reporters should track whether other Shore districts issue similar communications and investigate whether immigration enforcement activity is affecting enrollment or student attendance.

---

## Alternative Educational Models

The emergence of microschool and hybrid homeschooling options represents a new dimension of Shore education coverage. Beyond Words Tutoring, founded by special-education teacher Lindsey Parte, launched a hybrid homeschooling microschool in Stevensville beginning September 2024, serving 6-11-year-olds with blended schedules and low student-to-teacher ratios. Reporters should monitor whether these models are growing and what they reveal about parental satisfaction with traditional public schooling.

---

=== notes.md ===
Star-Democrat Choice Assignment
November 12,  2025

---

I chose option 2, generating a draft beat book, because I felt mostly satisfied with my entities assignment and I really wanted to try creating a more narrow "source list"-style beat book. I also was curious to see how Groq's models would perform, compared to more mainstream ones like Claude Sonnet 4.5.

## First Idea/Fail
Before committing to my idea of generating a beat book in the style of a source list, I tried — and failed — to port my old CNS beat book agent to work with Groq models instead of Claude, and the Easton Star-Democrat's education stories instead of CNS'.

This was a spectacular fail, mainly due to the strict limits Groq places on token input/output and the rate you're able to generate things with its models. I tried using multiple Groq models and rearchitecting several parts of this "agentic" setup with Copilot, but after over a dozen attempts I couldn't generate even one beat book. I even tried to use Copilot to set up a very illegal dynamic Groq API key system, where once one Groq API key's rate limit was reached, the agent would automatically switch to another one, but I couldn't get this to work either (frankly, that's 100% deserved).

Something else I noticed while trying this failed approach was that the Groq models I tested just aren't as good at tool calls as the mainstream models like Sonnet 4.5. While Sonnet would explore hundreds of stories by the end of its run (calling dozens of tools in the process), the Groq models I tested would explore anywhere from 0 to 50 stories and prematurely "end" their agentic processes without actually completing their tasks. There were also strange syntax issues with certain models' tool calls — particularly with GPT OSS 120B, which ocassionally outputed a `<think>` tag *inside* the arguments of a tool call, which would cause an error that would crash the entire agent. I could not figure out a way to prevent this, other than only using non-reasoning models. That was when I ultimately gave up on this approach (at least for now).

I gave up on trying this before I was able to test changes to the prompt, or change the sample of stories from the CNS ones (from my original "beat book agent" experiment) to ones from the Star-Democrat — so the prompt I used was the same as the one I used with the CNS stories:
```
Create a narrative guide in Markdown format that explains, using examples and lessons from the story database, what is important for an early-career journalist who knows the basics to understand about that topic, including the key people, issues and institutions.

Taking into account the kinds of stories have been done, suggest other story ideas or undercovered angles. Introduce the journalist to the topic as if you were an expert guide. Be careful about stories that are not resolved, such as ongoing lawsuits or policy debates; include a caveat that these might already be completed, but identify unfinished stories. Focus on the future more than the past; this is more about story ideas than history. Give more weight to more recent coverage.

The language should be business casual, and it should not be primarily a list of bullet points, but you can use bullet points for checklists or proposed sources or locations for a suggested story angle. If a timeline is important for the overall topic, mention that, but don't over-emphasize older elements.

Make sure you introduce and explain ideas, people and issues with substantive but not overwrought language. Not too many adjectives. Include citations to stories, including links, that provide useful context. Try to include the contact information of sources, only if possible.

## TOOL USAGE STRATEGY

Follow this workflow to explore the data efficiently:

**Phase 1: Overview (1-2 tool calls)**
- Start with `get_dataset_overview` to understand what data is available
- Use `analyze_coverage_patterns` for the topic to see what themes dominate

**Phase 2: Deep Exploration (5-10 tool calls)**
- Use `query_stories` with different filters to find relevant stories:
  - Filter by topic to get all stories in your beat
  - Filter by high follow-up ratings (7+) to find promising angles
  - Search by specific keywords for themes that emerged in Phase 1
- Use `get_story_details` on 5-10 of the most promising/representative stories
- Use `find_coverage_gaps` to identify what hasn't been covered

**Phase 3: Pattern Analysis (2-4 tool calls)**
- Use `get_institutional_analysis` to understand key organizations
- Use `get_geographic_distribution` to see coverage areas
- Use `analyze_story_themes` to identify recurring topics

**Phase 4: Synthesis**
- Write the beat book using insights from all tool calls
- Include specific citations with links from stories you examined

Remember: Query strategically. Each tool call should answer a specific question about the beat.
```

*Also, for some reason the Copilot thread I used to attempt this seems to have disappeared. I'm not really sure what happened to it, but I cannot find it anywhere — sorry about that!! In the future I'll be sure to paste them into my notes right after I finish using them.*

## Second Idea/Still a Fail?
I found a little more success when I lowered the scope of what I aimed to accomplish. I wanted to explore creating a "source list" beat book, which would be particularly focused on listing the top sources and people in the education beat at the Star-Democrat. I thought that limiting the scope would theoretically make the model better at performing its task, since it wouldn't have to focus on as much at once.

In total, I generated seven iterations for this beat book draft. I have to say, though, I'm really not happy with any of them.

To start off the process, I asked Copilot to create a script that would compile a list of the top-mentioned people in the Star-Democrat's education stories, sourced from my entities data from our earlier assignment. It created a Python script that outputted a text file called `people_counts.txt`, which contained a list of the most frequently mentioned people in the education stories and the number of times they were mentioned across all stories (in descending order).

I then created my `generate_beatbook.py` script, which I also asked Copilot to write for me. Essentially I asked it to create a system where Python code would "research" each person by searching through all the education stories for their name (basically doing "CMD + F" for people's names inside stories' content), and then this "research" would get passed to the LLM. I didn't want to pass the entire education topic JSON into the prompt because it would go over Groq's input token limits, so I figured the script could take the list of the most-frequently mentioned people, do the research on each one automatically and then give the LLM all that pre-written context to put into a beat book.

Unfortunately, this all resulted in supbar results. In the earlier iterations, the model I used (GPT OSS 120B) severely hallucinated its information, to the point where I was convinced I accidentally picked a less capable model like OSS 20B. It even didn't seem to understand that the Easton Star-Democrat is a proper name — it titled the beat book the "Education Beat Book" for the "East on Star‑Democrat" (two separate words). I did like the baseline format it established in its output though, of having a spreadsheet-like view of the source list at the top, and then more detailed info. about the top people in the education beat below. My goal after this iteration was to make it more accurate in subsequent iterations.

I asked Copilot to help with this, which ironically was able to verify by itself that much of the information had been hallucinated. What seemed to help over the course of my iterations was giving the model more context — I figured out that the original script Copilot made only gave the model the ability to read/access a maximum of two stories per source/person, rather than allowing it to read the content of *all* the stories they were mentioned in. It was trying to conserve tokens, which was a good goal, but it had become a little too conservative. In general, there was a lot of fighting back-and-forth between lowering token usage and giving the model enough information, since Groq's limits are so strict. This was never a problem with Claude (and I admittedly haven't worked much in my personal time with non-frontier models), so learning how to balance this is still an ongoing process for me. Even in my seventh iteration, I feel like I wasn't able to give the model enough information.

For my seventh (and so far final) iteration, this was the prompt I ended up using (thanks to Copilot), where I made the model role play as a senior news editor onboarding a new reporter:
```
You are a senior news editor at the Easton Star-Democrat, a local newspaper covering Maryland's Eastern Shore. You are creating a BEAT BOOK for a new education reporter.

CRITICAL INSTRUCTIONS - READ CAREFULLY:
- I have conducted COMPREHENSIVE research on each person, analyzing ALL articles where they appear
- For each person, I've detected potential roles and organizations by analyzing context across multiple articles
- CAREFULLY READ the detected roles and organizations - these are extracted from the actual text
- Cross-reference information across multiple articles to confirm consistent details
- Extract titles and roles that appear repeatedly or in formal contexts (like "Superintendent X announced..." or "Board President Y said...")
- DO NOT make up phone numbers or addresses
- Mark suggested contact info clearly as "[Suggested - verify before use]"
- Use the actual quotes provided to show the person's voice and perspective
- Synthesize information ACROSS articles - if someone is called "Superintendent" in 3 different articles, that's their confirmed role

COMPREHENSIVE RESEARCH DATA:
Below is detailed research from our archive. I've analyzed every article mentioning each person and extracted:
- Detected roles (patterns found across articles)
- Detected organizations (mentioned in context)
- Direct quotes (actual statements they made)
- Key article excerpts

{research_text}

YOUR TASK:
Create a beat book with entries for each person. For each entry, provide:

1. **Name** (exactly as found in articles)
2. **Role/Title** - Synthesize from the detected roles and article contexts. If multiple articles consistently refer to someone as "Superintendent" or "Board President," state that as their role. If genuinely unclear, note it.
3. **Organization** - Use the detected organizations and context
4. **What We Know** - Synthesize key facts across ALL their article appearances: what they've done, said, voted on, led, etc.
5. **Key Topics** - What issues they're connected to based on article analysis
6. **Quotes** - Include 1-2 actual quotes from the research (shows their voice)
7. **Contact Info** - I've conducted web searches for each person. Use the "Web search contact info" section if available. If actual contact info was found, include it. If not found, suggest likely email patterns based on organization, clearly marked as "[Suggested - verify before use]"
8. **Reporting Tips** - Based on their role and topics, suggest what angles to approach them about

CONTACT INFORMATION INSTRUCTIONS:
- If "Web search contact info" shows actual email addresses, INCLUDE THEM in the contact section
- Verify the emails look legitimate (proper domain for the organization)
- If no web search results, suggest standard patterns like firstname.lastname@organization.domain
- Always mark suggested (non-verified) contacts as "[Suggested - verify before use]"

FORMATTING:
- Title: "# Education Beat Book - Easton Star-Democrat"
- Subtitle: "## Compiled November 16, 2025"  
- Brief intro explaining this is based on comprehensive analysis
- Use ## for each person's entry
- Order by frequency (most mentioned first)
- Include disclaimer about verifying contact info

REMEMBER: You have COMPREHENSIVE data - use it! Synthesize across articles to build complete, factual profiles.

FORMATTING:
- Title: "# Education Beat Book - Easton Star-Democrat"
- Subtitle: "## Compiled November 16, 2025"
- Brief intro explaining this is based on article frequency analysis
- Use ## for each person's entry
- Order by frequency (most mentioned first)
- Include a disclaimer that all contact info needs verification

REMEMBER: Accuracy over completeness. If you don't see it in the excerpts, don't include it or clearly mark it as needing verification.

Generate the beat book now:
```

I will say, I think having Python code automatically research each person from the `people_counts.txt` file (by searching through all their article mentions) and then feeding that compiled research to the LLM could be an effective strategy. If I had more time, I would've tried to figure out how to better improve things like web search — I was trying to get Copilot to give the LLM a web search tool, but instead I think it tried building some kind of really basic scraper that would deterministically find people's contact info. instead? (Honestly, it's 3 a.m. and I'm too exhausted to understand what Copilot did there, but it definitely isn't what I was aiming for). I was hoping web search could be used by the LLM to verify and find the contact information of sources, but in every single iteration the model wrote placeholder emails and caveats like "[verify this]," which is useless for a new reporter.

Overall, I was let down by the Groq models' capabilities with this assignment, but I definitely want to keep exploring with them. In the future, I want to try letting the LLM do its own research through tool calls rather than making that part deterministic — I think that would be a more flexible approach, especially if I can figure out how to work within Groq's token limits better.

=== notes_v1.md ===
Star-Democrat Draft Beat Book
November 17, 2025

---

## Beat book
My beat book draft can be accessed in the `education_beat_book_refined_v1.md` file.

## Beat book topic choice
I'm going to try again with the education topic for this assignment — I really want to see if I can find ways around the Groq model context/rate limits for the topic's 629 stories.

## Beat book format
After trying a beat book that was styled more like a contacts list (or source guide), I'd like to try again with a more narrative-style beat book. Ideally I'll end up with something similar to the CNS beat book I created a while back, where the model mostly writes in paragraphs (as opposed to lists) and there are narrative throughlines throughout the beat book. I liked how my CNS one read a bit like an article, so I'm trying to emulate that here.

## Method
I ended up choosing a chunking-based method to load small chunks of stories (in their entirety) into a Groq model, chosen in a random order. Essentially, OpenAI-OSS-120B is given a number of stories (usually 2-5) from the 629 total stories in the education beat, and it's asked to write a preliminary beat book based off of those few stories. In its prompt, it's also given some meta-knowledge about its task in relation to the overarching goal of compiling a more cohesive/far-reaching beat book across all the chunks. Notably, it's also fed the previous beat book generation so it can get a sense of the previous chunks' stories — the prompt asks the model to iterate on that beat book. So in a way, we're creating a living document that the model updates after each chunk.

I also added a Claude-based "refinement" step, which uses Haiku to refine the writing of our beat book since I couldn't get the OSS-120B model to write in a way that matched my ideal style/tone. I found that OSS-120B frequently made small errors in its writing, like saying the "East Easton Star-Democrat" and mispelling people's names, so adding a refinement step with a smarter/larger model felt necessary. *(I was very careful about not giving Claude access to the actual data, since I know we are avoiding that. The OSS-120B-generated beat books felt deritivative-enough that I figured this was okay, but let me know if you'd rather I don't do this in the future!)*

Claude was a given a slightly broader view of our process in its prompt — I tried to give it a roleplay scenario where I gave it the role of being a "senior editor" whose job is to review the work of multiple junior writers and combine them to be more cohesive. I also told it to cut things from the beat book it felt were unimportant, in case the OSS-120B model wrote overly specific things about stories it was fed.

## Beat book web app
I also created a quick web app with Copilot to read the resulting beat book, with an AI panel on the side that lets the user ask GPT/Claude/Gemini questions about the beat book. I haven't tested this thoroughly but it seems somewhat useful!

## Results
I was able to iterate through 270 of the 629 stories (43% of the total education beat) before 4 p.m. on Wednesday. This process took a very long time (over 24 hours), though there were several pauses when Groq would ocassionally rate limit me — or ocassionally ban my account (sorry to our friends at Groq!)

Overall, the education beat book seems to be pretty good at a structural level. It provides county-by-county information about education coverage on the Eastern Shore, which I found to be useful. I also liked how at the end of the beat book it provided lists of sources and several stories the reporter should look into/follow up on. The factual information provided in the beat book seems accurate from what I can tell, but I'd be curious to know if Jim agrees.

I was generally impressed by the models' abilities to harmonize the information across so many different chunks of stories — I credit this mostly to clever prompting, including passing the previous iteration's beat book to the model, but I'm honestly not entirely sure how this worked as well as it did.

That said, the beat book is definitely on the longer side — it comes in at 30 pages as of Wednesday. I definitely should've changed the prompt in the "refinement" step to be more aggressive about cutting unnescessary (or less necessary) information. There are certain details in the beat book that don't seem important for a new reporter to know, like overemphasis on a story about two students at a high school who fought for a new illuminated digital sign for the school. I tried to "solve" this by adding the AI chatbot in the frontend interface I (Copilot) made, since the reporter could ask the chatbot to summarize things for them — but this is obviously a bandaid solution on a larger issue.

I'll plan on proceeding with this approach and iterating on my prompts to refine the models' capabilities with creating the beat book.

GitHub Copilot: Let me confirm I understand what you're asking:

You want a Python script that:

1. **Iterates through all 629 education stories** in source_stories.json in batches of 20 stories at a time
2. **Randomly selects** each batch of 20 stories (without replacement, so no story is sent twice)
3. **Maintains a "beat book"** (a guide for new reporters on the education beat) that gets progressively updated
4. For each batch of 20 stories, the script:
   - Sends the current state of the beat book + the 20 new stories to the Groq GPT-OSS-120B model
   - Asks the model to update the beat book based on what it learned from those 20 stories
   - The model should understand it's in an iterative process and can choose NOT to update if the stories don't add valuable information
   - The updated beat book becomes the new "current state" for the next batch
5. **Final output**: A comprehensive beat book that synthesizes insights from all 629 stories

The meta-awareness part means the prompt should explain to the model that it's seeing stories in chunks and should integrate new information into the existing beat book rather than replacing it entirely, and that it's fine to skip updates if nothing new/important emerges from a particular batch.

Is this correct?

GitHub Copilot: Created [](file:///workspaces/jour329w_fall2025/ludwig/stardem_draft/build_beat_book.py)

Created `build_beat_book.py` with comprehensive features:

**Key Features:**
- ✅ Processes stories in random batches of 20 (configurable)

python build_beat_book.py --reset

- `education_beat_book.txt` - The final beat book
- `beat_book_state.json` - Progress tracking (which stories processed, current beat book state, batch number)

Perfect! I've added graceful handling for the 413 token limit error. Here's what the system now does:

## Key Features Added:

1. **Error Detection**: Detects `Error code: 413` and `rate_limit_exceeded` in stderr
2. **Token Parsing**: Extracts the limit (e.g., 8000) and requested tokens (e.g., 8740) from the error message using regex
3. **Smart Trimming**: 
   - Calculates how much to reduce (targets 85% of limit for safety margin)
   - Preserves header (first 5 lines)
   - Preserves recent additions (last 20% of lines)
   - Intelligently trims middle sections proportionally
   - Adds a note explaining the temporary trim
4. **Automatic Retry**: Up to 3 attempts with progressively trimmed content
5. **Clear Feedback**: Shows token statistics and trimming percentage to user

## Example Flow:

```
Sending batch 5/50 to Groq model...
Token limit exceeded: Limit=8000, Requested=8740
Trimming beat book by ~23% to fit within token limit...
Retry attempt 2/3 with trimmed beat book...
[Success - continues with trimmed version]
```

The trimmed content is temporary - only used for that specific API call. The full beat book is preserved in state, so when the next batch runs, it gets the complete context again. If the beat book continues to grow, it will trim more aggressively on subsequent errors.

Made changes.

The application consists of three files:

### How to Run
Because the application fetches a local markdown file (`education_beat_book_refined.md`), you cannot simply open `index.html` in your browser due to security restrictions (CORS). You must run a local web server.

You can do this easily with Python from the app directory:

1.  Open a terminal.
2.  Navigate to the app directory:
    ```bash
    cd ludwig/stardem_draft/app
    ```
3.  Start a simple server:
    ```bash
    python3 -m http.server
    ```
4.  Open your browser and go to `http://localhost:8000`.

### Features
*   **Split View**: The Education Beat Book is rendered on the left using Markdown styling. The AI chat is on the right.
*   **AI Expert**: The AI is prompted to be an expert on the Easton Star-Democrat education beat.
*   **Tool Calling**: The AI has a `read_beat_book` tool that allows it to read the full content of the markdown file to answer questions accurately.
*   **Multi-Model Support**: You can switch between **GPT 5.1**, **Claude Haiku 4.5**, and **Gemini 3 Pro Preview**.
*   **Settings Modal**: Click the gear icon to input your API keys. These are saved locally in your browser's `localStorage`.

**Note**: Since this is a client-side application, you may encounter CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) errors when calling the APIs directly from the browser, depending on the provider's security settings. In a production environment, you would typically route these requests through a backend proxy.
